Italian consonant length stays at 0.62 to 0.63 whatever the number of examples: more examples do not help what is not heard.

Reading. The single example was a large part of the gap, as read at 00:36: five examples of each kind are worth 8 to 10 points, the same size as the cost of changing the speaker. With them and four listeners, place and manner are over the bar on natural read sentences in a corpus the listeners never saw; voicing and vowel quality are 4 to 7 points short, and an unheard language is 2 to 8 points short in every family. The frozen target, one example of each kind, stays unreached and this does not replace it.

### More speech from more voices. Statement (07:57)

Everything on disk left one-shot discrimination across speakers at 0.70 to 0.86, and no change of network, loss or distortion moved it; the one thing that did was the amount of speech (2.0 h to 22.8 h: +3 points in Spanish and Italian, +7 on LibriSpeech). The corpus the listener's speech came from has a far larger part never used: the train split of `changelinglab/cv-v1.0-segment` (2,372 shards, 174.9 GB, 4.73 million utterances, from the public size listing). With the user's yes, 71 shards were fetched at revision `a5ec8b8a` (4.97 GB): 11 of Bashkir, 10 each of Belarusian, Catalan, German, English, French and Kinyarwanda. The split is sorted by language and, within a language, by speaker, so the shards were taken at even steps through each language's range. Spanish and Italian shards were not fetched and no Spanish or Italian utterance is fitted on. Read from the small columns only: 172 h and at most 2,000 speakers, against 22.8 h and about 1,500 speakers before. The new speech is uneven: 23 shards hold one voice each, the English ones 26 to 147 each. Hours grow 8.6 times, voices about 2.3 times. German and Belarusian are new to the listener.

Idea and theory. A frame's name is the same whoever says it, so every further voice saying a phone is a constraint that removes the voice from the 64 numbers. The earlier slope was against hours with voices growing in step; here hours grow much faster than voices, so if voices are what count, the gain will be nearer the slope for 2.3 times than for 8.6 times.

Kept fixed: the installed listener's network, crop, batch, rate schedule, input, the split rule (speakers with `share % 20 == 0` kept out), the three tests (stage two, one example of each kind, X from another speaker; Spanish, Italian, LibriSpeech dev-clean), their triplets. Two fits, each one change from the installed listener: `more` (the same 6,000 updates on all the speech) and `long` (24,000 updates on it, since 6,000 updates of 96 three-second cuts pass over 195 h only 2.5 times, where they passed over 22.8 h 21 times).

Predictions, mean of voicing, place, manner and vowel quality (installed: Spanish 0.736, Italian 0.728, LibriSpeech 0.789):
- `more`: Spanish 0.74 to 0.76, Italian 0.73 to 0.75, LibriSpeech 0.80 to 0.82 (0.6 that all three rise; 0.3 that at least one falls, the fit being short).
- `long`: Spanish 0.76 to 0.78, Italian 0.75 to 0.77, LibriSpeech 0.82 to 0.85 (0.6).
- No family reaches 0.90 in any of the three under either fit (0.9). Nearest: manner on LibriSpeech, 0.863 now, 0.88 to 0.90 under `long`.

Branches fixed now. If `long` gains under 1 point on all three means, amount of speech at this size of listener is spent: FAILED, the lever is closed and the target is reported unreached. If it gains 2 points or more on all three, it replaces the installed listener after the convention and word tests show no loss, and a wider listener on the same speech is the next single change. Between, it is recorded and not installed.

### Where the long fits run, and a wider listener beside them. Statement (09:03)

Measured before choosing: 6,000 updates of the recurrent listener take 61 minutes on this machine (the `voices` fit, and `more` at the same pace), so `long` would take four hours here. The user then rented a machine for the fits (three Tesla P100 cards of 16 GB, 72 cores, 62 GB of memory). One forward and backward pass of the two recurrent layers at the fit's batch takes 0.128 s on one card, four to five times this machine's pace. `long` therefore runs there, on the same frames copied over (no audio or text leaves this machine beyond the frames and phone labels already extracted), with PyTorch 2.6.0 for CUDA; its draws differ from a fit on this machine, so `more` (fitted here) and `long` differ in machine as well as in updates. Scoring stays on this machine, on the same triplets as every earlier listener.

With a second card free, the single change that the statement of 07:57 named as next is fitted beside `long` instead of after it, at no cost in time: `wide`, the same listener with its convolutional and recurrent layers 512 wide in place of 256 (four times the weights), still 64 numbers a frame, 24,000 updates on the same speech. Theory: with 9 times the speech and far more voices, the 256-wide listener may no longer have room to name every frame; if so the wider one names more frames right and tells phones apart better, and if not the two score alike and size is not the limit.

Predictions for `wide` against `long`, mean of four families: higher by 0.5 to 1.5 points on at least two of the three tests (0.5); within half a point on all three (0.35); no family at 0.90 under either (0.9).

Outcome for `more` (09:14), for the statement of 07:57. Fitted on this machine in 61 minutes: 133,838 utterances, 201.5 h, 133 symbols; frame accuracy on its kept-out speakers 0.724 at the last update and still rising (the installed listener's 0.756 is on a different, smaller set of kept-out speakers and is not comparable). Stage two, one example of each kind, X from another speaker:

| Test | listener | voicing | place | manner | vowel | mean |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| Spanish | installed (22.8 h) | 0.750 | 0.726 | 0.716 | 0.751 | 0.736 |
| | `more` (201.5 h, same 6,000 updates) | 0.738 | 0.730 | 0.735 | 0.741 | 0.736 |
| Italian | installed | 0.696 | 0.729 | 0.750 | 0.737 | 0.728 |
| | `more` | 0.704 | 0.733 | 0.744 | 0.752 | 0.733 |
| LibriSpeech dev-clean | installed | 0.734 | 0.800 | 0.863 | 0.760 | 0.789 |
| | `more` | 0.745 | 0.830 | 0.867 | 0.780 | 0.805 |

Predictions: Italian (0.733, range 0.73 to 0.75) and LibriSpeech (0.805, range 0.80 to 0.82) HELD at the low end; Spanish FAILED (0.736, unchanged, range 0.74 to 0.76), so "all three rise" FAILED. No family at 0.90: HELD. Nine times the speech at the same number of updates buys 0 to 1.6 points: each utterance was passed over 2.5 times and the frame accuracy had not levelled, so this fit says little about the speech and much about its length. `long` is the test.

### Learning in a fixed time: a race between listener shapes. Statement (09:29)

The user asked for more learning, faster. Profiled first, on this machine at the fit's batch (96 cuts of 300 frames): one update of the recurrent listener takes 0.48 s, of which its two convolutions take 0.04 s; the recurrent layers are 92% of the time. The convolutional listener as fitted earlier takes 0.13 s an update, the recurrent layers at every second frame 0.27 s, at every fourth 0.17 s. Earlier scores already say the recurrent layers are not buying their cost on languages nobody fitted on: at 6,000 updates on 22.8 h the convolutional listener scored 0.765, 0.759, 0.785 (Spanish, Italian, LibriSpeech) against the recurrent one's 0.736, 0.728, 0.789. The rented machine stopped answering at 09:24, so `long` and `wide` are out of sight for now and the race runs here.

Protocol, fixed before the first run. Every entrant fits for 180 s on this machine's GPU on all 201.5 h (`race.py`), alone on the GPU, its rate rising over the first tenth of the time and falling to nothing at the end, so a faster shape makes more updates and all end their schedule together. Scored on speakers kept out of fitting only: frame accuracy, and stage-two discrimination among kept-out Catalan and English speakers (`abx.py ca|en <name> dev`, mean of four families; the recurrent listener after its full hour scores 0.847 and 0.720 there). Spanish, Italian and LibriSpeech are not looked at for choosing. The best entrant by the mean of the two goes on to a longer staged fit and only that is scored on the three tests.

Entrants: `deep` (the installed shape), `conv` (five convolutions as fitted earlier), `tall` (eight dilated convolutions, 64 numbers a frame), `tall2` (the same at every second frame from its second layer), `slow2` and `slow4` (recurrent layers at every second and fourth frame). Then the winner with each cut drawn from a speaker chosen evenly: a third of the new speech comes from 23 voices, and a voice heard again teaches less than a new one.

Predictions: a convolution-only entrant has the best mean after 180 s, at least 5 points over `deep` (0.7); `tall2` is ahead of `tall` and `slow2` ahead of `deep` (0.6 each); drawing by speaker adds at least 1 point to the winner (0.5).

Outcome of the race so far (09:49), for the statement of 09:29; the two half-rate recurrent entrants failed on utterances of odd length (their repeated frames came out one short), were fixed and are rerunning. After 180 s each, kept-out speakers:

| Entrant | updates | hours heard | frame accuracy | Catalan | English | mean |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `deep` | 360 | 28.8 | 0.547 | 0.789 | 0.663 | 0.726 |
| `conv` | 1,246 | 99.7 | 0.590 | 0.830 | 0.696 | 0.763 |
| `tall` | 774 | 61.9 | 0.580 | 0.800 | 0.671 | 0.735 |
| `tall2` | 1,288 | 103.0 | 0.601 | 0.800 | 0.690 | 0.745 |
| the recurrent listener after its hour (22.8 h, 6,000 updates) | | | 0.756 | 0.847 | 0.720 | 0.783 |

A convolution-only entrant is best (HELD) but by 3.7 points over `deep`, not 5 (FAILED); `tall2` is ahead of `tall` (HELD).

What the table says beyond the race: three minutes of the five-convolution listener is within 2 points of the recurrent listener's hour, and frame accuracy and discrimination have come apart. From 0.590 to 0.756 of frames named right, discrimination moves 0.763 to 0.783. Naming frames better is not what is missing.

### The comparison is not what the fit optimises. Statement (09:50)

Theory. Two tokens are compared by the cosine between their frames. The fit asks only that a small network after the 64 numbers can name the frame; it puts no price on a direction of those 64 numbers that varies with the voice or the neighbours and that the naming network has learned to ignore. The cosine does not ignore it. If so, the 64 numbers already hold a better comparison than the cosine reads out, and a linear map found from the fitting speakers alone should show it: scale every direction by how much the same phone varies along it (whiten the within-phone scatter), so that directions in which one phone wanders count for little.

Test, on the installed listener, no refit: from 1,500 utterances of fitting speakers drawn evenly over speakers (never the kept-out ones, never Spanish or Italian), the scatter of frames around their phone's mean; the map is its inverse square root. Scored on kept-out Catalan and English speakers with frames mapped before the cosine (0.847 and 0.720 unmapped; 0.864 and 0.747 by the root of the naming chances).

Predictions: the mapped frames gain at least 1.5 points on the mean of the two (0.5); if they do, the same map gains on Spanish, Italian and LibriSpeech too, since it is found from other languages (0.6 given the first). If the gain is under half a point, the cosine was not the leak and a fit whose loss is itself the cosine comparison is the next thing to try, not this.

Outcome (09:52), for the statement of 09:50. The map was found from 1,500 utterances of 1,445 distinct ones over 3,396 fitting speakers (532,045 spoken frames, 71 phones; the scatter within a phone is 283 times larger along its largest direction than its smallest). Kept-out speakers, installed listener, mean of four families:

| Frames compared | Catalan | English | mean |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| the 64 numbers as they are | 0.847 | 0.720 | 0.783 |
| whitened within a phone | 0.822 | 0.694 | 0.758 |
| whitened, then the 40 directions that part the phones most | 0.842 | 0.703 | 0.772 |
| root of the naming chances | 0.864 | 0.747 | 0.805 |

FAILED: whitening loses 2.5 points and the discriminant directions lose 1.1. The directions in which one phone varies most are not noise to be scaled down; scaling them down throws away what tells phones apart. The cosine over the 64 numbers was not the leak.

### One number a token, sharpened by what the listener names. Statement (09:52)

What the tables do say: the root of the naming chances is the only readout that beat the 64 numbers (+2.2), and the naming chances are the one thing that keeps improving as frames are named better. And five tokens of each kind are worth 8 to 10 points (01:22), which is what averaging away the accidents of a single token buys. A phone token of three to ten frames is compared frame by frame along a warping path, so every frame's accident counts. Pooling a token's frames into one row before comparing averages those accidents inside the token: the mean of the rows, or for naming chances the geometric mean, which is what the frames jointly say if each is taken as separate evidence.

Test on kept-out Catalan and English speakers, installed listener, no refit: tokens compared by the cosine between pooled rows (`pool=mean` on the 64 numbers and on the root of the chances, `pool=geo` on the root of the chances), against the warped comparison above.

Predictions: geometric pooling of the naming chances beats their warped comparison (0.805) by at least 1 point (0.5); pooling the 64 numbers does not beat their warped comparison (0.6), since the 64 numbers move through a phone and their mean blurs that.

Outcome (09:55), for the statement of 09:52. Kept-out speakers, installed listener, tokens compared by the cosine between one pooled row each:

| Frames | comparison | Catalan | English | mean |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|
| the 64 numbers | warped | 0.847 | 0.720 | 0.783 |
| the 64 numbers | mean row | 0.844 | 0.733 | 0.789 |
| root of the naming chances | warped | 0.864 | 0.747 | 0.805 |
| root of the naming chances | mean row | 0.869 | 0.743 | 0.806 |
| root of the naming chances | geometric mean | 0.867 | 0.748 | 0.807 |

FAILED: geometric pooling gains 0.2 points over the warped comparison, not 1. Pooling the 64 numbers gains 0.6 (the prediction that it would not beat warping FAILED by that much). How a token's frames are combined is not where the points are.

Where the misses are, from every trial of the installed listener written out (`abx.py … trials`; Catalan and English kept-out speakers, and LibriSpeech, whose trials were only broken down, nothing chosen on them): accuracy is flat in the shortest token's length (Catalan 0.84 at 30 to 50 ms, 0.86 at 50 to 150 ms; LibriSpeech 0.76 rising to 0.83) and in whether the two reference tokens differ in length; no pair is near 1.0, and pairs a listener should find easy sit at 0.69 to 0.84 across speakers (p/t: Catalan 0.84, LibriSpeech 0.79, Spanish 0.79, Italian 0.69) and 0.86 with the same speaker. The misses are spread over everything. The two large measured costs remain the change of speaker (9 to 14 points, 00:36) and the single token (8 to 10 points, 01:22).

### Take the speaker's average out of the frames. Statement (09:55)

Theory. If a voice shifts every frame of an utterance the same way in the 64 numbers, that shift is in the utterance's own average and can be taken out with no knowledge of the speaker: subtract each utterance's mean row from its frames before the cosine (and, as a second variant, also divide each of the 64 numbers by its spread over the utterance). The input bands already get this treatment; the 64 numbers do not. The whitening of 09:50 scaled directions by a scatter pooled over all speakers and lost; this removes a per-utterance offset instead, which a pooled scatter cannot see.

Test on kept-out Catalan and English speakers, installed listener, no refit: `centre` and `standard` as further arguments to `abx.py`, on the 64 numbers.

Predictions: centring gains at least 1.5 points on the mean of the two (0.45); standardising gains no more than centring (0.6). If centring gains under half a point, the voice is not an offset in these numbers and the remaining routes are a stronger listener or more than one token.

Outcome (09:57), for the statement of 09:55. Kept-out speakers, installed listener: each utterance's mean row taken out, mean of the two languages 0.788 (+0.5); also divided by the spread, 0.787 (+0.4); mean taken out of the root of the naming chances, 0.800 (−0.5 against 0.805). FAILED: under the 1.5 points predicted and at the half point fixed as the bound. The voice is not an offset in these numbers.

The race of 09:29 complete (09:57). After 180 s each, kept-out speakers, mean of Catalan and English, by the 64 numbers (or the last layer) and by the root of the naming chances:

| Entrant | updates | frame accuracy | by the numbers | by the naming chances |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `deep` (recurrent, every frame) | 360 | 0.547 | 0.726 | 0.743 |
| `slow2` (recurrent, every second frame) | 550 | 0.615 | 0.736 | 0.760 |
| `slow4` (recurrent, every fourth) | 876 | 0.635 | 0.738 | 0.755 |
| `tall` (eight dilated convolutions, 1.2 s in view) | 774 | 0.580 | 0.735 | 0.759 |
| `tall2` (the same at every second frame) | 1,288 | 0.601 | 0.745 | 0.759 |
| `conv` (five convolutions, 0.37 s in view) | 1,246 | 0.590 | 0.763 | 0.780 |

`slow2` is ahead of `deep` (HELD, +1.0). For scale, by the naming chances the installed listener scores 0.805 after its hour on 22.8 h and `more` 0.813 after its hour on 201.5 h (0.772 by its 64 numbers, under the installed listener's 0.783: its English rose 3 points by the naming chances with ten times the English).

Reading. The entrant that names frames best (`slow4`, 0.635) is not the one that tells phones apart best (`conv`, 0.590 named, 2 to 2.5 points ahead on both readouts). What separates `conv` from all the others is how little it sees: 37 frames around the frame it names, where the recurrent ones see the whole cut and `tall` 121 frames. A listener that sees far can name a frame from what the language usually puts there, which raises frame accuracy on the fitted languages and says nothing about the sound; one that sees only the sound's neighbourhood has to name it from the sound. That would also be why the recurrent listener lost to the convolutional one in Spanish and Italian yesterday and not on kept-out speakers of fitted languages.

### How far a listener should see. Statement (09:57)

If the reading is right, discrimination has a best width of view and it is short. Same race, same protocol, five convolutions of 256 each, only the dilations changed: `near3` (three layers, 13 frames in view), `near5` (dilations 1,1,1,1,1: 21 frames), `conv` (1,2,4,1,1: 37 frames, already run), `far5` (1,2,4,8,1: 69 frames).

Predictions, mean of Catalan and English kept-out speakers by the naming chances (`conv` 0.780): `far5` below `conv` by at least half a point (0.6); `near5` within a point of `conv` either way (0.5); `near3` below `conv` by at least 1.5 points, 0.13 s being too little to hold a stop's closure, burst and the vowel's onset (0.6).

Outcome (10:08), for the statement of 09:57. After 180 s each, kept-out speakers, mean of Catalan and English:

| Entrant | frames in view | updates | frame accuracy | by the last layer | by the naming chances |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `near3` | 13 | 1,675 | 0.556 | 0.754 | 0.770 |
| `near5` | 21 | 1,073 | 0.571 | 0.766 | 0.775 |
| `conv` | 37 | 1,246 | 0.590 | 0.763 | 0.780 |
| `far5` | 69 | 1,078 | 0.603 | 0.760 | 0.774 |

`far5` below `conv` by at least half a point: HELD (0.6). `near5` within a point: HELD. `near3` at least 1.5 below: FAILED (1.0 below). The width of view between 13 and 69 frames moves discrimination by a point at most, and `near5` and `far5` made 14% fewer updates than `conv` in the same time (the machine was busier), which is as large an effect. The reading of 09:57 is not supported as stated: seeing little is not what puts `conv` ahead; what the five-convolution listeners share against the others is pace. No best width was found worth a longer fit.

Judged together (margins summed, by the naming chances), kept-out speakers: the four convolutional three-minute listeners 0.791 (+1.1 over the best of them), all eight racers 0.786, the installed listener with `more` 0.826 (+1.3 over `more` alone). Listeners together add about a point here; not a lever.

Every change so far lands between 0.78 and 0.83 on kept-out speakers: shape, width of view, ten times the speech, the comparison, pooling, whitening, utterance centring, listeners together.

### Canonicalise the voice at the input. Statement (10:11)

The change of speaker costs 9 to 14 points and nothing done to the frames after the listener recovers it. Fitting the listener on distorted voices (00:08) asked it to ignore a stretch of the frequency axis and moved nothing. The other classic route is to undo the stretch before listening: a longer or shorter vocal tract moves every resonance by one factor, so for each utterance try a few stretches of the band axis and keep the one under which the listener is most sure of its naming (mean log of each frame's highest naming chance). No refit, no label, no knowledge of the speaker; nine listenings an utterance.

Test on kept-out Catalan and English speakers, installed listener, by the naming chances (0.805 without): `abx.py … vtln`, stretches 0.88 to 1.12 in steps of 0.03.

Predictions: at least 1.5 points gained on the mean of the two (0.4); the chosen stretches differ between speakers more than within a speaker's utterances (0.7), which is the check that it finds a property of the voice. If the gain is under half a point with consistent stretches per speaker, the listener's frames already absorb vocal tract length and the speaker cost lies elsewhere.

Outcome (10:14), for the statement of 10:11. Kept-out speakers, installed listener with each utterance heard under its surest stretch: by the naming chances 0.805 (0.805 without), by the 64 numbers 0.784 (0.783). The chosen stretches spread 0.062 (Catalan) and 0.071 (English) over utterances and 0.043 and 0.039 within a speaker: they are partly a property of the voice (HELD), and undoing them gains nothing (FAILED). The listener's frames already absorb a stretch of the frequency axis; the cost of a new speaker is not vocal tract length.

Seven changes that needed no refit or a three-minute one have now been tried on kept-out speakers and none is worth more than a point. What is left is the listener itself: its size, and how long it is fitted on the 201.5 h.

### A faster machine, and the size of the listener. Statement (10:14)

The first rented machine went offline at 09:24 with `long` at about 1,500 and `wide` at about 600 updates; nothing of them was kept. The user rented another (one L40 card of 46 GB, 96 cores). Measured there at the fit's batch: the recurrent listener 30 updates a second (this machine 2.1, the P100 5.5), 512 wide 17, 1,024 wide in half precision 12; the convolutional listener 90. A fit that took an hour here takes four minutes there, so the size of the listener can be read off a ladder within the hour.

Ladder, all on the same 201.5 h, 24,000 updates, same batch, rate schedule and split, recurrent listener with 64 numbers a frame: `long` (256 wide, as installed), `wide` (512), `huge` (1,024, fitted in half precision). Statements of 07:57 and 09:03 stand for `long` and `wide`. Scoring is on the rented machine with the same script and frames, so the same triplets; kept-out Catalan and English speakers first, by the naming chances and by the 64 numbers, then Spanish, Italian and LibriSpeech.

Predictions for `huge` against `wide`, kept-out speakers by the naming chances: higher by at least 1 point (0.4); if `wide` is not above `long` by half a point, `huge` is not above `wide` by half a point either (0.7). No family at 0.90 on any of the three tests for any rung (0.85).

Branch. If the ladder climbs by a point or more a rung, size is a lever and the next rung and more updates follow. If it is flat, neither the amount of speech nor the size of this kind of listener is the limit, and what remains inside the goal's rules is a different kind of learning signal, not a bigger fit.

### The most from the least: a bench of one taught hour. Statement (10:19)

The user's direction at 10:15: many short experiments, raising ability with as few examples as possible; never scale a dead end; scale only what works small. The size ladder stated at 10:14 is therefore not run: ten times the speech bought 0 to 1.6 points, and a ladder on all of it would be scaling that.

The bench, fixed before its first run. The teacher names one hour of speech, the same utterances for every entrant (all fitting utterances of the original 22.8 h in a fixed order, cut at one hour); the other 21.8 h may be heard without names. Each entrant fits for 60 s on the rented L40 card and is scored on speakers kept out of everything: share of frames named right, and stage-two discrimination among kept-out Catalan and English speakers by the 64 numbers and by the root of the naming chances (mean of the two languages by the naming chances is the number compared). Spanish, Italian and LibriSpeech are not looked at. The baseline runs with three seeds first, to know how much a difference must be to count. An entrant given all 22.8 h of names for the same 60 s is the yardstick: how much of the gap from one hour to all of them does an entrant close.

Entrants, each one change from the baseline (the recurrent listener, one named hour): `spread` (the hour drawn one utterance from every speaker before a second from any), `voices` (every cut distorted as another voice might give it), `self` (half of every batch is unnamed speech named by the finished baseline), `selfsure` (the same, keeping only frames the baseline names with a chance of at least 0.5), `conv` (the five-convolution listener).

Theory and predictions. With one hour named, what is short is voices and tokens, not updates, so the changes that add variety without names should pay here even though distortion paid nothing with 22.8 h named. `self`: the baseline's wrong names on unnamed speech are partly unsystematic, and a fresh listener fitted on them with the true hour averages over 20 times more voices: at least 2 points gained and at least 30% of the gap closed (0.5). `voices`: at least 1 point (0.5). `spread`: at least half a point (0.4); the fixed order already draws the hour from several hundred speakers. `conv` at least 1 point over the baseline (0.4).

Outcome (10:38), for the statement of 10:19. Each entrant 60 s on the L40 (about 1,200 to 1,500 updates for the recurrent listener), kept-out speakers:

| Entrant | named | speakers named | frames named right | by the 64 numbers | by the naming chances |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| baseline, seed 0 | 1 h | 252 | 0.635 | 0.688 | 0.672 |
| baseline, seed 1 | 1 h | 252 | 0.636 | 0.696 | 0.685 |
| baseline, seed 2 | 1 h | 252 | 0.634 | 0.702 | 0.663 |
| `voices` | 1 h | 252 | 0.625 | 0.708 | 0.704 |
| `spread` | 1 h | 593 | 0.558 | 0.721 | 0.711 |
| `self` | 1 h (+21.7 h unnamed) | 252 | 0.659 | 0.714 | 0.717 |
| `selfsure` (71.5% of the teacher's frames kept) | 1 h (+21.7 h unnamed) | 252 | 0.658 | 0.719 | 0.707 |
| `conv` (7,474 updates) | 1 h | 252 | 0.570 | 0.680 | 0.657 |
| all names | 22.75 h | 1,483 | 0.738 | 0.761 | 0.783 |

The baseline's three seeds give 0.673 by the naming chances with a spread of 0.011 (0.695 and 0.007 by the numbers), so a difference under 2 points is not read. The gap from one named hour to all of them is 11.0 points.

`self`: +4.4 points, 40% of the gap, HELD (at least 2 points and 30%). `voices`: +3.1, HELD (at least 1). `spread`: +3.8, HELD (at least half), with a confound: taking one utterance from every speaker also moves the hour towards Catalan and English, which have most of the speakers and are the two languages scored (its frame accuracy over all languages fell to 0.558), so how much of it is voices and how much is language is not separated. `conv`: −1.6, FAILED (predicted at least +1): at one hour its 7,474 updates are 600 passes over the hour and it names kept-out frames worst. Keeping only the frames the teacher is sure of does not beat keeping all (0.707 against 0.717, inside the spread).

The yardstick itself is a finding about wall time: 60 s on this card with all names gives 0.783, where the installed listener's hour on this machine gave 0.805.

### Combining what paid, and less wall time. Statement (10:38)

The user's rule at 10:37: efficiency first, in examples and in wall time, then capability. Three changes each closed 3 to 4 points of an 11-point gap by different means (more voices in the named hour, each cut heard as other voices, unnamed speech named by a teacher), so if they are independent their gains add.

Entrants, same bench: `sv` (spread and voices), `svself` (spread, voices, and unnamed speech named by `spread`, the baseline for that hour), `self2` (unnamed speech named by `self`: the student as the next teacher), `selfv` (teacher hears the cut clean, the student hears it distorted), and for wall time `b20` and `self20` (the baseline and `self` with 20 s in place of 60).

Predictions, by the naming chances (baseline 0.673, all names 0.783): `svself` at least 0.74, two thirds of the gap (0.45); `sv` at least 0.72 (0.5); `self2` at least 1.5 points over `self`'s 0.717 (0.4); `b20` within 1.5 points of the baseline at 60 s, since the baseline is memorising its hour (0.6); `self20` at least 0.70 (0.5), which would make 20 s with a teacher better than 60 s without.

Outcome (10:47), for the statement of 10:38. Kept-out speakers, by the naming chances (baseline 0.673 ± 0.011, all names 0.783):

| Entrant | fit | named speakers | frames named right | by the 64 numbers | by the naming chances | of the gap |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `sv` (spread, voices) | 60 s | 593 | 0.573 | 0.715 | 0.710 | 34% |
| `svself` (spread, voices, unnamed speech named by `spread`) | 60 s | 593 | 0.596 | 0.742 | 0.751 | 71% |
| `self2` (unnamed speech named by `self`) | 60 s | 252 | 0.664 | 0.728 | 0.713 | 36% |
| `selfv` (teacher hears clean, student distorted) | 60 s | 252 | 0.664 | 0.733 | 0.732 | 54% |
| `b20` (baseline) | 20 s | 252 | 0.623 | 0.682 | 0.687 | 13% |
| `self20` (unnamed speech named by the baseline) | 20 s | 252 | 0.647 | 0.708 | 0.723 | 45% |

`svself` at least 0.74: HELD (0.751; one named hour is 3.2 points from 22.75 named hours). `sv` at least 0.72: FAILED (0.710, no more than `spread` alone at 0.711: spreading and distorting do not add). `self2` at least 1.5 over `self`: FAILED (0.713 against 0.717): a second generation of teacher adds nothing. `b20` within 1.5 points of the 60 s baseline: HELD (0.687, if anything above: 40 more seconds on one hour buy nothing). `self20` at least 0.70: HELD (0.723): 20 s with a teacher is ahead of 60 s without, and no worse than 60 s with one.

What adds: the teacher's naming of unnamed speech adds to either of the other two (`selfv` +1.5 over `self`; `svself` +4.1 over `sv`). What does not: a second generation, more seconds, a sureness cut, spread with distortion.

### Fewer names, fewer seconds, softer names. Statement (10:47)

Entrants: `in` (the hour spread over speakers inside each language, every language keeping its share of the plain hour: separates voices from the move towards Catalan and English); `svself20` (the three-part formula in 20 s); `svsoft` (the formula with the student fitted to the teacher's chances for every symbol on unnamed speech, not only its likeliest name: the teacher's doubt between two phones is information the likeliest name throws away); and a quarter of an hour named: `q0` (baseline, 20 s), `qspread` (spread, 20 s), `qsvself` (formula, 60 s, unnamed speech named by `qspread`).

Predictions, by the naming chances: `in` at least 0.69, half of `spread`'s gain kept (0.5); `svself20` at least 0.735 (0.5); `svsoft` at least 1.5 points over `svself`'s 0.751 (0.4); `qsvself` at least 0.70, fifteen named minutes under the formula ahead of a named hour without it at 0.673 (0.45).

### Does the teacher's gain reach languages never heard? Statement (10:54, written while the scoring ran, before any of its numbers were read)

The bench scores kept-out speakers of Catalan and English, and the unnamed speech the teacher names holds 9 hours of Catalan and English. So the gain from `selfv` (teacher hears clean, student distorted; +5.9 points on the plain hour) may partly be getting used to those two languages, which is fair for a learner that can listen to a language before being taught it, and worth nothing for a frozen expert meeting a language cold. Check, once: the three baseline seeds, `selfv` and the all-names yardstick (all 60 s fits) on Spanish, Italian and LibriSpeech dev-clean, stage two as always. This is a check of a chosen entrant, not a choice among entrants.

Predictions, mean of the three tests by the naming chances: `selfv` above the baseline's mean of three seeds by at least 2 points (0.5); its gain there smaller than its 5.9 on kept-out speakers (0.7).

Outcome (10:57), for the statement of 10:47. Kept-out speakers, by the naming chances:

| Entrant | named | fit | named speakers | frames named right | by the 64 numbers | by the naming chances |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `in` (spread inside each language) | 1 h | 60 s | 270 | 0.629 | 0.691 | 0.674 |
| `svself20` | 1 h | 20 s | 593 | 0.582 | 0.732 | 0.740 |
| `svsoft` | 1 h | 60 s | 593 | 0.595 | 0.737 | 0.744 |
| `q0` (baseline) | 15 min | 20 s | 90 | 0.560 | 0.672 | 0.622 |
| `qspread` | 15 min | 20 s | 156 | 0.555 | 0.667 | 0.643 |
| `qsvself` | 15 min | 60 s | 156 | 0.604 | 0.702 | 0.695 |

`in` at least 0.69: FAILED (0.674, the baseline's score). Inside each language the hour was already nearly one utterance a speaker where speakers are many, so `in` adds 18 speakers and nothing else; what `spread` changed was the share of Catalan and English in the hour. Its 3.8 points are therefore read as teaching more of the two scored languages, not as voices, and `spread` leaves the formula. `svself20` at least 0.735: HELD (0.740; the formula loses 1 point going from 60 s to 20 s). `svsoft` at least 1.5 over `svself`: FAILED (0.744 against 0.751). `qsvself` at least 0.70: FAILED by half a point (0.695), though fifteen named minutes under the formula are ahead of a named hour without it (0.673), with the same caveat about `spread`.

What stands on the plain hour, with no help from the choice of languages: unnamed speech named by a teacher, the student hearing it distorted (`selfv`, 0.732, +5.9 points, 54% of the gap to 22.75 named hours).

### How the formula behaves with more and fewer names, a better teacher, a wider student. Statement (10:57)

Entrants on the plain order of utterances (no `spread`): `c4` (baseline, four named hours) and `c4selfv` (the formula on them, teacher `c4`); `qselfv` (fifteen named minutes, teacher `q0`); `selfv3` (the named hour, unnamed speech named by the three baseline seeds together, their chances averaged: three teachers err differently, so their average names more frames right than any one, which is information the student cannot get from one teacher and which a second generation did not supply); `selfvw` (the student 512 wide); `selfv20` (20 s).

Predictions, by the naming chances: `c4selfv` at least 2 points over `c4` (0.5), a smaller gain than at one hour (0.7); `qselfv` at least 4 points over `q0`'s 0.622 (0.5); `selfv3` at least 1.5 points over `selfv`'s 0.732 (0.4); `selfvw` within a point of `selfv` (0.5); `selfv20` at least 0.72 (0.5).

Outcome (11:12), for the statement of 10:57. Kept-out speakers, by the naming chances, 60 s unless said:

| Named | baseline | with the formula (`selfv`) | gain |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 15 min (90 speakers) | 0.622 (20 s) | 0.685 | +6.3 |
| 1 h (252 speakers) | 0.673 ± 0.011 | 0.732 | +5.9 |
| 4 h (777 speakers) | 0.716 | 0.760 | +4.4 |
| 22.75 h (1,483 speakers) | 0.783 | not run: no unnamed speech left in this pool | |

`c4selfv` at least 2 over `c4`: HELD (+4.4), and smaller than at one hour: HELD. `qselfv` at least 4 over `q0`: HELD (+6.3). `selfv3` (three teachers together) at least 1.5 over `selfv`: FAILED (0.742, +1.0, inside the spread; it also made 873 updates against about 1,190, three teachers costing time). `selfvw` (512 wide) within a point: HELD (0.735). `selfv20` at least 0.72: HELD (0.724, against 0.732 at 60 s).

Read across the rows: fifteen named minutes with the formula (0.685) are level with a named hour without it (0.673), a named hour with it (0.732) is ahead of four named hours without (0.716), and four named hours with it (0.760) are 2.3 points from 22.75 named hours without. On this bench the formula is worth about four times the names, and 20 s of fitting is within a point of 60 s.

First part of the check stated at 10:54 (Italian and LibriSpeech timed out with ten scorings at once and are rerunning). Spanish, a language none of them heard named or unnamed, mean of four families by the naming chances: baseline seeds 1 and 2 0.665 and 0.666, `selfv` 0.727, all 22.75 h named 0.754.

### More speech to listen to. Statement (11:12)

The 71 train-split shards were fetched again on the rented machine from the same address and their frames made there by the same script (the upload from here ran at under 1 MB a second); three shards compared give the same number of frames as here. They join the bench as speech heard without names only (`listen`): the named utterances and the kept-out speakers stay those of the original 22.75 h, so every earlier line stays comparable. Unnamed speech goes from 21.7 h to about 215 h, with 2,000 more voices and two languages nobody names (German, Belarusian).

Entrants: `l1` (one named hour, formula, listening to all), `l4` (four named hours), `lall` (all 22.75 h named, unnamed speech named by the all-names yardstick `ball`: the formula where it could not be tried before).

Theory. In 60 s the student hears about 48 h of unnamed cuts whatever the pool, so a larger pool does not give it more speech, only more variety: every cut new, many more voices. The teacher's naming is no better. Predictions, by the naming chances: `l1` at least 1 point over `selfv`'s 0.732 (0.4); `l4` at least 1 over `c4selfv`'s 0.760 (0.4); `lall` at least 2 points over the yardstick's 0.783 (0.5), the gain continuing its fall from 5.9 at one hour to 4.4 at four.

Outcome (11:27), for the statement of 11:12. Kept-out speakers, by the naming chances, 60 s, unnamed speech 200.5 h in place of 21.7 h:

| Named | baseline | formula, 21.7 h unnamed | formula, 200.5 h unnamed |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 h | 0.673 | 0.732 | 0.745 |
| 4 h | 0.716 | 0.760 | 0.766 |
| 22.75 h | 0.783 (again 0.784) | | 0.782 |

`l1` at least 1 point over `selfv`: HELD (+1.3, about the size of the seed spread). `l4` at least 1 over `c4selfv`: FAILED (+0.6). `lall` at least 2 over the yardstick: FAILED (0.782 against 0.783 and 0.784). Ten times the listening adds a point at most, and with every name given the formula adds nothing. So the formula replaces names, about four for one; it does not raise what the listener reaches with all of them, which stays at 0.78 after a minute.

The check stated at 10:54, as far as scored (Italian timed out twice with too many scorings at once and is rerunning three at a time). Languages never heard, named or unnamed, by the naming chances:

| Test | baseline, 1 h named (seeds 1, 2) | `selfv`, 1 h named | all 22.75 h named |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Spanish | 0.665, 0.666 | 0.727 | 0.754 |
| LibriSpeech dev-clean | 0.662, 0.663 | 0.722 | 0.786 |

`selfv` at least 2 points over the baseline: HELD on both (+6.1, +6.0). Its gain smaller there than the 5.9 on kept-out speakers: FAILED on both, and welcome: the gain is not a matter of getting used to the scored languages, it carries whole to a language the listener never heard in any form.

Standing after five batches of one-minute fits. Wall time: a minute on the rented card reaches 0.783 where an hour here reached 0.805. Names: a teacher naming unnamed speech with the student hearing it distorted is worth four times the names, at 15 minutes, 1 hour and 4 hours, and on unheard languages. Not moved by anything: what a listener of this kind reaches with all names, 0.78 to 0.81.

### Cues that lie outside the phone. Statement (11:27)

What the comparison is given is the frames inside the phone's aligned interval. For several of the scored contrasts the telling cue is not there: a nasal's place is carried mostly by the movement of the neighbouring vowel's resonances into and out of it, a stop's place by its burst and those same movements, its voicing by when the vowel's voice starts after the release. The listener's frames inside the interval can hold some of that, having seen the neighbours, but it was fitted to name the phone, not to keep the neighbours' evidence. Earlier comparison changes re-weighted the same frames and gained nothing; this one adds frames. In stage two the following phone is the same for A, B and X, so taking a few frames of it into each token adds the movement out of the phone without adding a difference that is not the phone's.

Test on kept-out Catalan and English speakers, installed listener, no refit: each token lengthened by 3 or 6 frames into what follows (`after=3`, `after=6`), by the 64 numbers and by the naming chances (0.783 and 0.805 with none).

Predictions: `after=3` gains at least 1.5 points by the 64 numbers (0.4), most for place (0.6 given a gain); by the naming chances it gains less than by the numbers (0.6), since frames past the boundary are named as the shared following phone and only dilute. If neither gains half a point, the evidence the comparison lacks is not sitting just past the boundary.

Outcome (11:30), for the statement of 11:27. Kept-out speakers, installed listener, tokens lengthened into what follows: by the 64 numbers 0.780 with three frames and 0.760 with six (0.783 with none); by the naming chances 0.787 and 0.776 (0.805 with none); three frames of what precedes, 0.761 and 0.798. FAILED: nothing gained by the numbers, and the naming chances lose 1.8 points. The evidence the comparison lacks is not sitting just past the boundary; frames outside the phone only dilute.

On this machine three minutes put the convolutional listener 3.7 points ahead; on the card it makes 90 updates a second to the recurrent listener's 30.

If 240 s is not at least 1.5 points over 60 s for any shape, fitting longer is closed as a lever along with more speech.

### Very fast training: what 20 seconds can reach. Statement (12:05)

The user's direction at 12:05: many short experiments, one at a time, nothing long until training is shown to be very fast. A staged 24,000-update fit begun at 12:05 on the card was stopped within the minute, unscored. The unit from here is one 20 s fit on the card with every name given, scored on kept-out Catalan and English speakers, read, and only then the next. The mark to beat is the five-convolution listener at 20 s: 0.778 by the naming chances, 0.762 by its numbers, 1,567 updates (the recurrent listener needs 240 s for 0.817). Each line below is stated before it is run.

1. The mark again with another seed, for the spread at 20 s. Prediction: within 1.5 points of 0.778 (0.8).
   Outcome: 0.788 by the naming chances, 0.773 by its numbers (1,598 updates). HELD. The mark is 0.783 ± 0.007 (0.768 by the numbers) from two seeds; differences under 1.5 points are not read.
2. Peak rate 0.01 in place of 0.004: in 1,600 updates a listener with normalised layers can usually take larger steps. Prediction: at least 1.5 points over the mark (0.3); within 1.5 either way (0.5).
   Outcome: 0.778 by the naming chances, 0.766 by its numbers (1,670 updates). No gain: the size of the steps is not what limits 20 s.
3. In 20 s the listener hears 128 h of cuts, under two thirds of the speech once: it is short of speech heard, not of passes. `quick`: five convolutions with all but the first at every second frame (64 numbers a frame, repeated back to every frame), to hear nearly twice the speech in the same time. Predictions: at least 1.6 times the updates (0.8); at least 1.5 points over the mark by the naming chances (0.35).
   Outcome: 0.786 by the naming chances, 0.768 by its numbers, and 1,576 updates: no more than the full-rate listener. More updates: FAILED, and that is the finding. The card is not what sets the pace at 80 updates a second; putting each batch together on the processor is (96 cuts taken from 133,000 separate arrays, widened, and sent over, every update).
4. `bank`: every frame and its name are placed on the card once and each batch is cut there. Same five-convolution listener, same 20 s. Predictions: at least 1.2 times the updates (0.7; the convolutions alone measured 90 a second); at least 1.5 points over the mark (0.25).
   Outcome: 2,239 updates (1.4 times, HELD), 0.796 by the naming chances and 0.777 by its numbers: +1.3 over the mark, under the 1.5 set, so the score is read as level and the pace as gained. `bank` stays on for what follows; the mark with it is 0.796.
5. `quick` with `bank`: now that the card sets the pace, the half-rate listener should make the updates it could not make before. Predictions: at least 1.5 times the updates of line 4 (0.7); at least 0.81 by the naming chances (0.35).
   Outcome: 2,162 updates, no more than line 4 (FAILED), 0.783 by the naming chances and 0.773 by its numbers (FAILED). Two listeners of very different cost make the same 108 updates a second, so the pace is still set outside the card: by the loop itself, which draws each batch's utterances from a list of 133,000 numbers that is turned into an array at every update.

Halving the frames inside the listener does not halve its time: at this batch the card spends its time starting small operations, not computing, so a listener this small cannot use the card with batches this small.

### One representation from both listeners. Statement (12:24)

Shipping two listeners with consumers choosing between them would push a research finding (which listener suits which task) onto every consumer. Simpler: the expert hears with both and gives one row a frame, the recurrent listener's 64 numbers and the convolutional listener's 256 side by side, each at unit length and the pair scaled so the whole is at unit length. The cosine between two such rows is then the mean of the two listeners' cosines, so every consumer that compares rows (telling apart, finding, reading a convention, learning words) uses both without knowing it.

Two pairings are tried on the choosing material only (kept-out Catalan and English speakers; Welsh second and Ukrainian first and second vocabularies, six archive views with the joined rows counted three times): the installed recurrent listener with `conv240`, and `deep240` with `conv240`. The better goes to the three tests and the convention test once.

Predictions for the better pairing: on kept-out speakers within half a point of `conv240`'s 0.794 by the numbers, or above (0.6); examples needed within 3 of the best arrangement so far on each vocabulary (Welsh second 50.0, Ukrainian 17.5 and 22.5) (0.5); on the three tests within 1 point of `conv240` or above (0.6).

A second hearing is worth as much as before below 0.38 and little above; the measured table is in `acting.py`. Reading the Italian loss again: the hearings repay (0.984 against 0.935 right for 0.016 of cost); what loses is 136 questions on utterances whose convention was fully taught (109 with the old rows), each costing the answer and 0.15. That is the reader marking a reading open when it is not, and the table of hearing gains does not touch it.

Statement (12:50). Teach what a name says, not only the name.

Idea. A phone's name is one of 133 unrelated answers: nothing in the teaching says that b and d share a manner, that b and p share a place, or that Catalan ɛ and English ɛː are nearly one sound. The alphabet itself says all of it. Give every named frame, beside its name, what its symbol says of the sound: 33 yes-or-no statements (voiced, labial, alveolar, stop, fricative, nasal, high, front, rounded, long, and so on; `features.py`, written by hand from the IPA chart, marks such as ː, ʲ and ʰ adding their own). Each statement is taught by every frame of every symbol that carries it, so a rare symbol's sound is learned from the common ones, and the layer that is scored is asked for exactly the distinctions the families of the test are made of. This is teaching, on the labelled side of the comparison, and uses no speech or model from outside.

Theory. With an hour of names, each of 84 symbols has on average 43 seconds, and the rare ones a few; each of the 33 statements has minutes to tens of minutes. A layer that must answer both is pulled towards directions shared across symbols, which are the ones a new speaker's phone must fall along to be told from its neighbour.

Test: `race.py` with `features`, the five-convolution listener, 20 seconds on the card, an hour of names, two seeds with and without; and once with all names. Scored on kept-out Catalan and English speakers by both readouts. Predictions: with an hour of names the layer's numbers gain 2 to 4 points and the naming chances 1 to 3, on both seeds (the spread between seeds is about a point); with all names the gain is under 2. If the gain with an hour is under a point on both readouts, the idea is closed at this dose and not scaled.

Outcome (12:55), second part of the statement of 12:46: the Italian streams under the measured gains.

| All four syllables taught, 2,880 utterances | answer at once | three hearings | `phonetics/act`, earlier gains | `phonetics/act`, measured gains |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| right among the answered | 0.935 | 0.984 | 0.984 | 0.980 |
| further hearings an utterance | 0 | 2 | 0.54 | 0.34 |
| questions | 0 | 0 | 136 | 133 |
| net of costs | 0.935 | 0.924 | 0.914 | 0.918 |

Right in at least 0.95: HELD. Net of costs at least 0.930: FAILED (0.918). The measured table saves a third of the hearings for 0.4 points of right answers. The loss to answering at once is the 133 questions. A probe on Spanish with the joined rows (5,760 fresh syllables) shows why: after a full teaching the reader marks 11.6% of readings open, and after a partial one 57.8% of the untaught syllables; demanding that both halves choose with a margin of 0.2 takes the first to 4.1% and the second to 35.0%, and a cap on the whole reader's margin does no better. No cut separates a half that misreads from a convention that is open, one utterance at a time. Standing: with an open convention `phonetics/act` gains (untaught syllables after its questions 0.894, whole streams net 0.780 against 0.742); with a settled one it loses 1.7 points net to answering at once, and the package says so. What would tell the two apart is the share of readings marked open over the stream, which the rule does not yet see.

Outcome (12:55), for the statement of 12:50 (what a name says). Twenty seconds, an hour of names, kept-out speakers:

| | updates | layer's numbers | naming chances |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| names, seed 1 | 2,490 | 0.691 | 0.667 |
| names and what they say, seed 1 | 1,892 | 0.682 | 0.680 |
| names, seed 2 | 2,499 | 0.690 | 0.680 |
| names and what they say, seed 2 | 1,968 | 0.681 | 0.673 |

FAILED: −0.9 and −0.9 on the layer's numbers, +1.3 and −0.7 on the naming chances, and a fifth fewer updates in the time. Closed at this dose and not scaled. Telling the listener what the symbols share does not make it hear them better across speakers; the names already carry it, or the hour is short of voices and not of statements about sounds, which is what the gain from distorted voices and a teacher's names on unnamed speech (5.9 points at the same hour) says.

With all names the same holds (20 seconds, 201.5 h): names 0.776 by the layer's numbers and 0.796 by naming chances from 2,499 updates; names and what they say 0.764 and 0.785 from 2,087. Under 2 was predicted; it is a loss of a point.

Statement (12:56). More updates in the twenty seconds. With all names a 20-second fit is short of updates and not of speech: 2,499 updates give 0.776 / 0.796 where 20,261 gave 0.794 / 0.808. Three ways to more updates, each one change against that entrant (`ha`: five convolutions 256 wide, `bank`, seed 1): `tuned` (the library times its convolution routines and keeps the quickest; cuts are always 96 by 300), `tuned half` (half precision on the card), and a narrower listener (`slim` 128 wide, `mid` 192 wide; compute falls with the square of the width). Predictions: `tuned` 1.1 to 1.3 times the updates and the score within a point; `half` on top 1.3 to 1.8 times and within a point; `slim` 2.5 times or more the updates and between −1.5 and +0.5 points; `mid` between the two. A way that gives 1.5 times the updates at no loss is kept for every later fit; a narrower listener is kept only if it is no worse here and no worse at 60 seconds.

Outcome (13:14), for the statement of 12:56 (more updates in the twenty seconds). All names, 20 seconds, kept-out speakers:

| Entrant | updates | layer's numbers | naming chances |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| 256 wide (`ha`) | 2,499 | 0.776 | 0.796 |
| `tuned` | 2,090 | 0.770 | 0.788 |
| `tuned half` | 1,936 | 0.769 | 0.779 |
| 128 wide | 2,976 | 0.774 | 0.786 |
| 192 wide | 2,832 | 0.782 | 0.792 |

FAILED on all three: timing the routines costs updates (0.84 times), half precision gives none (0.93 times), a listener half as wide gives 1.19 times and not 2.5. Nothing is kept. A profile of one update on the card says why (made frames, each stage timed to completion):

| Batch (cuts by frames) | precision | forward | backward | whole update | frames a second |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 96 by 300, 256 wide | full | 2.2 ms | 5.7 ms | 7.8 ms | 3.7 million |
| 96 by 300, 256 wide | half | 2.1 | 4.8 | 9.0 | 3.2 million |
| 96 by 300, 128 wide | full | 2.1 | 3.1 | 7.6 | 3.8 million |
| 96 by 300, compiled (two modes) | full | 2.0 to 2.4 | 5.6 to 6.0 | 7.3 to 8.2 | 3.5 to 3.9 million |
| 96 by 100 | full | 2.1 | 3.1 | 6.1 | 1.6 million |
| 384 by 300 | full | 15.2 | 28.2 | 43.7 | 2.6 million |
| 384 by 300 | half | 6.9 | 19.0 | 26.4 | 4.4 million |
| 1,152 by 100 | half | 7.2 | 20.2 | 27.8 | 4.1 million |

An update of 96 cuts has a floor of about 6 ms that neither width, precision nor compiling moves; larger batches pay in proportion, and half precision only helps them. The card moves about four million frames a second through this listener in every arrangement, which is eleven hours of speech a second: twenty seconds is one pass over the 201.5 hours and four minutes are thirteen. The fit is therefore not short of updates in a sense more throughput can mend. One pass gives 0.776 / 0.796 and thirteen give 0.794 / 0.808; 22.75 hours for a minute gave 0.768 / 0.782. Speech, passes and pace are each worth a point or two here, and the listener taught names levels off near 0.80 on kept-out speakers. The lever that paid today is the other one: what the hour of names is spread over (voices and a teacher's names on unnamed speech, 5.9 points).

Statement (13:20). One request from a sound to another package's expert.

Idea. The package's operations are so far asked for one at a time by a script that knows their order. An expert is part of the whole only if the kernel can put it on a route it plans itself, beside experts of other packages, from what a request gives to what it wants. Two operations are missing for that: `phonetics/understand`, from a taught reader and heard rows to a reading, and `phonetics/meant`, from a reading and a sequence of things of any one type to the thing chosen (the type is a variable of the contract, so the package names no other package). Then a request that gives a waveform, a taught reader, what each taught word stands for and whatever else an expert elsewhere needs, and wants that expert's result type, is a planning problem over manifests.

Test. The orientation expert of the independent grid-navigation package turns a panel seen and a heading into a stride (`relay.orient`, types `panel`, `heading`, `stride`). Four words of an unheard language are taught as the four headings, one to eight pointed-at examples a word. For every clip of those words from people the teaching never used, one request gives the waveform, the reader, the four headings and a panel, wants a `stride` and requires every given to be used; both packages are installed from wheels in the clean consumer process and the script imports neither expert. Welsh and Ukrainian third vocabularies, two groups of four words each, 160 clips a group.

Predictions. (1) The kernel plans listen, understand, meant, orient in that order from the manifests alone, and the result equals the orientation expert applied to the true heading in at least 0.80 of requests at one example a word and at least 0.93 at eight (chance 0.25). (2) A request takes under 30 ms at the median. (3) With the same audio and the headings of two words exchanged in the teaching, the same fresh utterances of those words give the other stride in at least 0.90 of the cases where the first teaching gave the right one. If the planner finds no route, the cause is reported and the contracts changed, not the kernel.

Outcome (13:24), for the statement of 13:20. `nca-phonetics` 0.8.0 adds `phonetics/understand` (a taught reader and heard rows to a reading) and `phonetics/meant` (a reading and a sequence of things of one type, a variable of the contract, to the thing chosen); 22 package tests pass, one of them the kernel planning from a waveform to a made type. Both wheels (this package and `nca-grid-navigation` 0.1.0) installed in the clean consumer process; `evaluation/act_through_another_expert.py` imports neither expert.

The route the kernel planned from the manifests, for a request giving a waveform, a taught reader, four headings and a panel and wanting a stride: `phonetics/listen`, `phonetics/understand`, `phonetics/meant`, `relay.orient`.

| Pointed-at examples a word | Welsh third (lowest to highest teaching) | Ukrainian third |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.898 (0.76 to 0.95) | 0.930 (0.89 to 0.97) |
| 2 | 0.920 (0.86 to 0.96) | 0.968 (0.94 to 0.98) |
| 4 | 0.966 (0.94 to 0.99) | 0.973 (0.95 to 0.99) |
| 8 | 0.978 (0.96 to 0.99) | 0.986 (0.97 to 0.99) |

5,120 requests a language, eight teachings (two groups of four words, four orders), 320 clips from people never taught from; chance 0.25.
1. HELD: the route is planned as predicted; right in 0.898 and 0.930 at one example a word (0.80 predicted) and 0.978 and 0.986 at eight (0.93 predicted).
2. HELD: 13.0 and 12.2 ms at the median, 21 ms at the 95th percentile; the process peaks at 539 and 515 MB.
3. HELD: with two words' headings exchanged in the teaching, 628 of 628 and 626 of 626 fresh utterances that had been acted on rightly give the other stride.
One pointed-at example a word is enough for nine in ten spoken words from a new speaker to reach the right act of an expert the phonetics package has never heard of.

Statement (13:25). Words from fewer pointed-at examples by also hearing without being told.

Idea. The formula that paid for the listener (a teacher's names on unnamed speech) applies one level up. A learner who has been pointed at eight examples is surrounded by more speech in the same language that nobody explains. Let it hear the rest of the teaching pool (the clips of the teaching speakers that have not been pointed at yet) with no meaning given, read each with what it knows so far, take its own readings as if they had been pointed at, and fit again before answering. No teacher effort is added; hearing time is.

Theory. With one or two examples a word the reader knows each word from one or two voices. Its readings of the unexplained clips are right far more often than not, and those clips bring the other teaching voices in; a ridge fit over likeness averages over the wrong ones as long as the right ones outnumber them.

A first look came before this statement was written, and is exploratory for that reason: the probe that was to reproduce the pointing-only counts printed the new arm beside them. On the three choosing vocabularies (six archive views with the joined listener counted thrice; mean examples needed over sixteen orders, and the lower block's share right at eight examples):

| | pointing only | and hearing the rest | its own readings right |
|---|---|---|---:|
| Welsh second | 52.0, 0.621 | 35.5, 0.653 | 0.862 |
| Ukrainian first | 19.0, 0.754 | 13.5, 0.824 | 0.918 |
| Ukrainian second | 25.0, 0.734 | 17.5, 0.790 | 0.930 |
| the six views alone (the control): Welsh second | 84.0 | 61.0 | 0.850 |
| Ukrainian first | 51.5 | 33.0 | 0.865 |
| Ukrainian second | 46.5 | 34.0 | 0.873 |

About three in ten examples saved for every reader. Trusting only the surer half of its readings twice over is worse (47.0, 15.5, 24.5); further rounds change nothing, since a clip fitted to its own reading reads the same again. The arrangement is fixed at: every unexplained clip of the pool, one round.

Predictions for the third vocabularies, which this arrangement has not been given (pointing only, through the kernel: control 26.0 and 13.5, control with listener 15.5 and 9.0; the smallest count tried is 8):
1. Control with listener, hearing the rest: Welsh at most 12.5 (a fifth under 15.5), Ukrainian 8.0 to 9.0.
2. The control hearing the rest: Welsh at most 21, Ukrainian at most 11.5.
3. Against the control given the same hearing, the listener still saves at least 25% in Welsh; in Ukrainian 20 to 35%, since both readers are near the smallest count.
If Welsh does not fall under 14, the gain on the choosing vocabularies does not carry and the arrangement is not put in the package.

Statement (13:29). Overhearing in the package's reader, measured where the kernel acts. `Reader.overhear` keeps a stretch nobody pointed at anything for; at reading time such stretches are read by what was pointed at and then count beside it under their own readings (the pointing alone still decides which reader answers and whether a reading is open). In the request from a spoken word to the orientation expert, the reader also overhears the teaching speakers' other clips of the four words (fifteen and fourteen a word at one and two examples a word). Predictions: at one example a word the share right rises from 0.898 to at least 0.93 in Welsh and from 0.930 to at least 0.95 in Ukrainian; at two, to at least 0.95 and 0.97; a request stays under 60 ms at the median. If the gain is under a point in both languages the method stays out of the package.

Statement (13:41). The middle of a token. The test compares every frame the corpus gives a phone. Its first and last frames are where the alignment is least sure and the neighbours most present; the 180 ms the second listener looks at already bring the transitions into the middle frames. Compare only the middle of each token (`abx.py … core=F` keeps the middle share F of the frames, at least one). No refit; kept-out Catalan and English speakers, `conv240`. Prediction: with the middle half the vowel family gains 1 to 2 points and the mean of four moves by less than a point either way; with the middle frame alone the mean falls. Kept only if the mean gains a point or more at some share on both languages.
